--- Using a cloud computing platform ---

Now, in this chapter, I want to give just a brief introduction when it comes onto cloud computing.
In this course, we're going to be looking at using services on Azure when it comes onto cloud computing
itself.
In this chapter, I first want to give a quick introduction.
Cloud computing is the delivery of compute services over the internet.
Here you get access to services that can allow you to deploy compute machines whose databases build
and deploy applications.
Initially on the Azure platform, we're going to see how to use the virtual machine service when it
comes to deploying compute machines.
Then later on, we look at other services like Azure Web Apps, Azure SQL databases, so on and so forth.
Now there are many benefits when it comes to using cloud computing platforms, and you'll understand
these benefits as we start using services on the Azure platform.
The first most important benefit is the cost.
See, when it comes all the cost, you're going to pay anything up front.
You pay only based on the usage.
Next is the speed at which you can deploy applications.
So you can deploy applications much faster because you are able to provision Provisioned infrastructure
on an on demand perspective.
And you get access to the latest hardware and software services.
Just some of the advantages when it comes onto cloud computing platforms.
You know, as an example, when it comes onto the costing aspect, before the advent of cloud platforms,
if a company had to deploy applications.
So let's say a company had to deploy an e-commerce application.
Obviously, the development team would write the relevant code and build the application.
But when it came time to deploy the application, the company would need to invest money in buying servers
and buying storage, setting up the network, setting up data centers.
All of this would cost money, so there would be an initial sum of money that the company would need
to invest in order deploy applications.
But one of the biggest benefit of a cloud platform is a company does not need to invest that initial
amount of money when it comes onto building infrastructure.
Right?

=== Describe Azure architecture and services - Identity and Access ===


--- Virtual Machines in Azure ---

Hi and welcome on to the section wherein we are going to work with Compute Services on the Azure platform.
So far we have a brand new Azure account in place.
It's just waiting for us to start using the various services.
We're going to start with a bread and butter service that's available on Azure.
And that is the virtual machine service.
I want to give a quick introduction first in this particular chapter.
And then we'll go through various labs on how we can use this service.
So what exactly is the virtual machine service?
This service allows you to host virtual machines without the need of buying and maintaining underlying
physical hardware.
Here you only pay based on the usage of the service itself.
Now, for those students who are not familiar with virtual machines, virtual machine is only a concept
that is been available.
You know, before the advent of the cloud, we could build virtual machines.
Just going back in the past again, just for those students to explain just a cool concept when it comes
onto virtual machines.
So let's say a company had to deploy an application.
They want to make an application available to users.
First of all, the company would need to buy hardware.
They would need to buy servers, they would need to buy storage.
And then they could host their applications.
In the past, long ago, in the past, you had this concept of companies buying physical servers on
these physical servers.
They would install the underlying OS.
This could be windows servers, this could be Ubuntu Linux a Linux distribution.
And then they could host their applications.
Then came the advent of virtualization on these physical servers.
You could install virtualization softwares.
And these virtualization softwares gave you now the ability to build individual virtual machines.
The entire purpose of having virtual machines.
I'm not talking about the cloud.
I'm talking about a company in the past, how they would host their applications.
So I said, first and foremost, they would buy physical servers.
They would buy physical storage.
They would need to host all of this in a data center, because they would need to have networking in
place.
Obviously electricity is required or cooling is required for servers, etc..
So obviously you need to have buildings in place to host these servers.
These buildings would need to have cooling in place for the servers, for the storage, etc..
And I said with the help of this virtualization software, you could start building virtual machines.
The entire idea of virtual machines was to make, you know, maximum use of the underlying hardware
of the servers.
So if you had a server that, let's say 16 CPUs and 64 gigs of Ram.
You could build one virtual machine that could take up, let's say, four CPUs of the underlying physical
server.
Another one that could take, let's say two CPUs, so on and so forth, and also take a portion of the
Ram as well.
So you have now many machines that can work in tandem, hosted on the same physical server.
These virtual machines can take up the physical attributes of the physical server itself.
And then you could host applications on the virtual machine.
So you're making maximum use of the underlying physical server.
But again the company needs to buy physical servers.
They need to buy physical storage.
They need to deploy the virtualization software, maintain it and then create the virtual machines.
But in Azure you don't need to buy or maintain the physical infrastructure.
Everything is managed by Azure themselves.
You can just start using the service to deploy virtual machines.
So let's go on to the next chapter.
Let's see how we can start building virtual machines on Azure.

--- Shutting down the virtual machine ---

Now, in this chapter, I want to go through the simple process of shutting down the virtual machine.
If you don't need the machine to be running right when you are running VM, you have to understand that
there is a cost associated with the running of the VM itself.
Let's say you want to save on costs when it comes onto the CPU of the machine itself.
You can go ahead and shut down the machine at any point in time.
Now if I go onto the VM, I have the ability from the Windows Server menu to perform a shutdown of the
machine here itself.
Now, when you do perform a shutdown of the machine at the OS level, yes, it will shut down the machine
itself, but then there is still a slight charge that is incurred for the machine.
If you want to perform a complete shutdown of the machine and not incur any sort of cost for the machine
itself.
You will need to go on to the Azure portal and go onto the machine itself in the overview.
You can perform the act of stopping the machine.
Now when you stop the machine from here, you are deallocating the machine.
And what do I mean by deallocating the machine?
So we have to remember that the virtual machine is hosted on some sort of physical server in an Azure
data center.
When we perform the operation of stop deallocate from the Azure portal, what this will do is it will
deallocate the VM from this underlying physical server.
When you start the machine again, the VM, the VM would be launched on either the same physical server
or another physical server, right?
This is all dependent upon Azure, but you just have to understand that if you want to make sure that
you are not charged, you know the running cost for the machine itself, you will need to perform the
act of stop deallocating the machine from the Azure portal itself.
Now, when you do perform the act of deallocating the machine, this is a point wherein the data on
the temporary drive is lost.
And what I mean by this.
So if I go on to my windows based machine and just open up the Windows Explorer.
So for the machine, if I go into this PC.
So I'm logged in onto the Azure VM, I have the OS level disk that's of size 127 gigs.
At the same time, I have also been assigned temporary storage in the form of a D drive.

--- Adding stability for your systems ---

Now, in the next chapters, we're going to go through features that allow us to increase the availability
of our systems at an infrastructure level.
And here we are focusing on our virtual machines.
But before that, before I go through these concepts, you know, we are going to look at something
known as availability sets.
We are going to look at something known as availability zones.
But before all of this, I want to go through the important concept as to why do we need to consider
these terms of high availability?
Why do we need to add some stability to our entire system?
Now consider that you have an application.
So let's say a company has an e-commerce application hosted on a virtual machine.
And your users from the internet who are accessing the web application itself.
Now, what happens if the entire machine that is hosting the application goes down?
The e-commerce application is no longer available.
Customers are dissatisfied.
You know, you lose customer confidence and you also have a loss in revenue as well.
See, Azure has a cloud platform does ensure high availability of their services, but from a platform
level, no one can guarantee a complete 100% availability for online services.
Also, you know your VM could go down for a variety of reasons.
There could be something wrong at the application level that could be causing problems at the virtual
machine level.
So there are so many cases as to why your VM can go down.
Businesses that have critical and revenue generating applications on the cloud or even in their on premises
environment, they always look at options into making their application Tolerant to failures.
So if there is a failure at the application level, there should be a process or a procedure on how
to recover from that failure.
If there is a failure at the infrastructure level, again there needs to be some process, something
that can be done to make the system, you know, highly available in nature and tolerant to such failures.
So you always hear terms like high availability, fault tolerance, so on and so forth.
At an infrastructure level, we can make our system tolerant to.
False.
False at the infrastructure level.
In the next chapters, we are going to go through two concepts known as available sets and availability
zones.
This helps to add kind of that stability at an infrastructure level when it comes onto your virtual
machines.

--- Availability Sets ---

Hi and welcome back.
So in this chapter I want to go through the concept of availability sets.
In the last chapter I talked about that overview of, you know, the need of adding stability to your
entire systems and an infrastructure layer when it comes on to your virtual machines.
We do have some features in place for adding, you know, higher availability to your infrastructure.
In this chapter, I want to go through the use of something known as availability sets.
So again, let's take the use case example where in a company is hosting an application on just a single
Azure virtual machine.
I told you, if you have a very critical application running on the machine, and if the machine goes
down, the application is no longer available, it can result in a loss of revenue and a loss of customer
confidence, so on and so forth.
Hence, you want to ensure that the application is always running.
Hence, you want to make sure that it's tolerant to faults at different layers.
Told you that the application can go down on its own due to several reasons.
The application could cause problems on the VM.
The VM could go down.
The VM can also go down for other reasons.
At the infrastructure layer.
See for example, we know that the virtual machine in the end is hosted on a physical server in an Azure
data center.
There could be faults in the underlying physical layer.
So there could be probably a network issue a power issue.
If this is the case, then it would cause a problem to the physical server and hence cause a problem
to the virtual machine.
I said Azure makes sure that their infrastructure is always, you know, highly available, but no one
can guarantee a complete 100% availability for infrastructure.
Although sometimes Microsoft has to perform vital updates to their physical machines.
These physical machines again are hosting your virtual machines.
Sometimes when these updates are applied, it would require a reboot of the entire physical server.
Now, if a reboot is required of the physical server, even though it is a fraction of a time, it could
just take a few minutes.
But in that few minutes, your application will no longer be available because your virtual machine
will be down.
Again, it's a problem for a company if they are having a critical application in place, a critical
application running on the VM.
So what companies normally do is they increase the availability of these critical applications by making
sure that they have multiple machines in place.
So they'll have the application running on multiple Azure VMs.
So if one machine were to go down, the application would still be available on another machine.
But the problem is that when you launch a VM, you have no control over what physical server the VM
is located on.
Let's say that both machines are launched on the same physical server in the Azure Data Center.
Again, we have that same problem wherein if there is a power failure that impacts this particular physical
server, both machines are down.
Or when Microsoft needs to perform an update on the physical server itself.
Again, both machines are down.
So what you can do is you can distribute your machines across your physical infrastructure by placing
them as part of something known as an availability set.
So what exactly is an availability set?
An And a baby set is a logical grouping of machines that helps you reduce the chances of multiple VMs
going down because of hardware issues.
To make use of availability sets, you need to deploy a machine to the availability set itself.
The machine can only be part of the set when it is created, so you can't add currently an existing
machine as part of an availability set.
So when you create the machine at that point in time, you have to make the machine part of an availability
set.
So how does availability sets actually help in the case of these power failures?
In terms of whenever Microsoft needs to perform an update on the underlying physical infrastructure.
So when a machine is made part of an available set, the machine is allocated on something known as
a fault and an update domain.
So A-b-b sets are made up of various fault and update domains.
What is a fault domain?
A fault domain defines a group of machines that share a common power source and network switch.
What are update domains?
Update domains are used to signify the group of machines and physical hosts that can be rebooted at
the same time.
So let's say that a company, you know, they have an application that is being used extensively.
Hence they have six machines in place just for the application itself to make sure that you have high
availability.
I said if you want to deploy these six machines as they were, maybe they might be located on the same
physical server and have the same problem at the infrastructure layer.
But if you make these machines part of an availability set, the available set has different fault and
update domains.
means.
So when the machines are made part of the available set, they are allocated to different fault and
update domains.
So let's say out of these six machines, two machines are part of fault domain zero.
So at the infrastructure layer let's say there's a problem with the power supply and Ethernet switch
of just for domain zero.
It would only impact these two machines.
It will not impact the other machines.
So at fault layer you are trying to isolate the issues when it comes on the power supply.
You know, things such as the power supply network such as etc. by placing the machines as part of different
fault domains and at the same time the machines are placed as part of different update domains.
So when Microsoft needs to publish updates to the physical servers, the updates are made to, you know,
each update domain at a time.
Obey domain zero could be one set of physical servers.
So when the updates need to go as part of this update domain, it only impacts this particular virtual
machine.
When the updates are done to this update domain, then the updates go on to the next update domain.
So this machine will be up and running and the updates will then impact this machine.
But in the end you still have machines available because they are part of other update domains.
When you create an available set, you can decide on the number of fault domains that you want and the
number of update domains.
Now note that there is no additional cost for using a baby sets.
Obviously from a costing perspective, right?
You are only again charged based on the number of machines that you have running as part of your entire
system.
Now, in the next update, I want to showcase how simple it is to go ahead and create machines as part
of an availability set.

--- Availability Zones ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now in the prior chapter, we discussed the concept of a query sets wherein, you know, we can spread
our machines across multiple fault and update domains.
And by doing so we are, you know, kind of distributing our machines across different power supplies,
Ethernet switches and making sure when updates are done, they are only carried out to a set of machines
at a time.
But what we need to remember is that in the end, the physical infrastructure when it comes onto an
available set, all of them are located within a single data center within a particular Azure region.
What if we need to make sure that our infrastructure is available, even if a data center were to go
down?
Again, this is not a very likely event, but let's say there's a power outage on the entire data center
takes time to get everything online again, your infrastructure is again not available.
Well, we can make use of something known as availability zones within Azure regions.
Not all of them, but most of them.
Regions have something known as availability zones.
What exactly is an availability zone?
This is a group of data centers.
There are fast links across the baby zones to ensure low latency.
So when you are now deploying your VMs, what you can do is insert deploying the VMs onto an availability
set.
You can deploy the VMs across different availability zones.
So for example, here you can have two machines that are part of one availability zone.
And a baby zone is made up of multiple data centers.
If the entire zone were to go down, you will still have machines available in the other zones in the
same Azure region again.
You'll be telling me, Alan, seriously, do we really need to consider building machines across different
zones?
I said, sometimes companies have these critical applications that generate revenue on a per second
basis, and every second that the infrastructure is down, the application is down.
They lose money.
So in such cases, companies do need to consider how do they make their applications highly available?
How do they make their infrastructure highly available?
Now again, when it comes on to using availability zones, there is no cost as such.
But there is a cost when it comes on to data transfer between a baby zones.
So if there is data being transferred across machines across a baby zones, then there is a data transfer
charge.
Let me go into showcase how simple it is to go ahead and just deploy a machine as part of an availability
zone.

--- Azure Spot Virtual Machines ---

Now in this chapter, in continuation to the pricing models when it comes on to virtual machines.
So we have the on demand pricing where we pay based on how much we use.
We also have something known as Azure Spot Virtual Machines.
Now Azure Spot Virtual Machines allows you to take advantage of the unused capacity that Azure has to
offer.
For example, you know, I told you that when it comes to VMs, in the end, these VMs are located on
physical machines.
Let's say that Azure has unused capacity in place in terms of physical infrastructure, and they want
to make use of this unused capacity.
Hence, they would allow you to build virtual machines on this unused capacity and also at the same
time save on costs.
So you can get significant cost savings by using Azure Spot virtual machines.
So if I try to quickly just showcase this.
So if I choose to create an Azure VM, you know, we've already seen the creation of the VM itself.
If I choose a resource group just give a name for the machine in North Europe, no infrastructure redundancy
required.
See here.
Now I can run with Azure Spot discount.
Now when I choose the option of run with Azure Spot discount.
It's tell me something known as an eviction type and an eviction policy.
Now, even though you know we are getting the benefit of using the unused capacity at a significant
discount, so what will happen is the price that we pay for running this instance will be less than
the price we would normally pay for on demand pricing.
But when Azure requires the capacity back then it will go ahead and evict your virtual machine.
That means you will lose your virtual machine.
So this is a risk, right?
You are getting a VM at a significant cost saving.
But at the same time, if Azure needs its capacity back, it can go and actually evict your Azure VM.
This is good when you have workloads that don't need to run 24 by seven, but if you have web applications,
you will probably not use these spot virtual machines.
If you have some batch jobs that need to run and they could run even if you lose the VM at a later point
in time, then you can consider the use of Azure Spot virtual machines.
But again, this is an option that you can use.
It's good for your batch processing jobs, your environments like dev and test that don't run a production
workload.

--- Virtual Machine Scale Set service ---

Welcome back.
Now I want to go through a service known as the Virtual Machine Scale set service.
Before that, let's go through some concept as to why would we want to make use of this particular service
known as virtual machine scale sets?
Now consider that a company has a web application running on a virtual machine.
So let's say this is an e-commerce application.
Users across the world are making use of the application.
Let's say that the load has increased.
You know, the application usage has increased and it's causing an issue on the, you know, a performance
issue on the underlying virtual machine.
Let's say that currently the virtual machine is of a particular size wherein it has four virtual CPUs
and eight gigs of Ram.
What we can do is we can resize the machine to give it more hardware resources in terms, in terms of
virtual CPU and in terms of Ram.
This is an example of vertical scaling wherein we are increasing the hardware on the machine itself.
Now, for some time, this should resolve probably the issues when it comes on to performance.
But when the load increases even more, you know there is a point at which this sort of scaling in terms
of vertical scaling will not help.
What you might need to do then is to perform the act of horizontal scaling.
Where in you now create multiple virtual machines, and then you make sure that your application is
hosted across these machines.
So now when users actually hit your application, the user load will be, you know, or should be distributed
across these machines.
The benefit of this is it also minimizes the downtime.
So when you resize your machine, there might be a requirement wherein the machine might need to be
restarted.
So during that time your machine will not be available.
Your application will not be available.
But here when you're adding more and more machines onto your fleet, your application is still working
on the existing virtual machines, you're only adding more machines to the fleet to take in the additional
user load.
But then how can we, you know, add these machines onto our architecture that has we need more and
more machines for the load.
How do we add the machines?
Is someone going to check every time whether the load is increasing, you know, using a monitoring
tool and then start adding more machines?
And you know, sometimes the traffic is not constant, right?
The traffic for applications, the load could be high during the daytime and then light during the night
time.
So is it that we need someone during the daytime to actually monitor and add more machines to the infrastructure?
And then again in the night to basically decrease the number of machines?
Because see, we should also make sure that we don't have too many machines running as part of our architecture.
Because on Azure, as you run more and more machines, you are adding more and more to the cost.
Because you have more machines running, you have more cost per individual machine.
Hence, if machines are not required as part of your infrastructure, they should not be running.
You only add machines whenever they are required.
So for this, what you can do is we can make use of a service known as Azure Virtual Machine Scale sets.
This service helps you to create and manage a group of load balanced virtual machines.
The number of virtual machines can grow based on demand or based on a schedule.
So now what you do is you use the virtual machine scale set to actually create your machines.
The user traffic can then be obviously distributed across these machines.
But then you can define rules or scaling conditions in the scale set, which can determine when to increase
the number of machines in the set and when to decrease the number of machines.
Let's go on to the next chapter to see how we can build a virtual machine scale set.

--- About Azure Web Apps ---

Now, in this chapter, I want to give an introduction onto Azure Web Apps.
And then in the subsequent chapter, we look at an example on using this particular service.
Now when it comes on to hosting a web application, we have a number of options in place when it comes
onto Azure.
What we can do is for a virtual machine, we can install a web server and then host our application.
When it comes onto a virtual machine, the underlying physical server is managed for you in the underlying
data center by the service itself.
But sometimes a company does not even want the hassle of maintaining even the virtual machine infrastructure.
They want to have a managed service for hosting the web application.
Well, for this they can make use of Azure Web App Service.
So this is a managed service wherein the physical server and the virtual infrastructure is managed for
you.
Here you only have to basically deploy your code on the web service and that's it.
You know, everything will be running in the background for you in terms of the virtual machine infrastructure,
in terms of whatever is required for running your application.
There is support for web applications that are based on dotnet Java, Node.js, PHP, and Python here.
Even the patching of the framework and the OS is managed by the service itself.
You also get additional features such as high availability.
So if you have a web application that fits the frameworks that are available and you don't want to manage
the underlying infrastructure, then you can opt to using the Azure Web App Service.
If you need to host a custom application, one that does not fit this framework, and maybe you need
complete control over the underlying virtual machine infrastructure, so you need admin rights over
the compute machine.
Then in that case, you can make use of virtual machines itself.
But if you want to manage platform for your web applications, we can make use of Azure Web apps.
So let's move on to the next chapter when we can look at using Azure Web Apps itself.

--- About Azure Functions ---

And welcome back.
Now in this chapter, I want to give an introduction when it comes on to Azure Functions.
Now let's say that you have a web application.
This web application could be hosted on a virtual machine.
The web application could be hosted on an Azure web app.
The web application could be making external calls onto other modules.
So you could be having a central repository of code, you know, central repository of services.
And each service is having its own functionality.
So it's not part of the web application.
As such, you might be having many web applications that are calling these centralized or need to call
these centralized set of modules.
So each module is code code that needs to be run, code that performs certain functionality at a central
level.
Okay.
So in your organization you might be having central services that provide common functionality right
to many applications.
Let's say that these modules only run on demand, right?
You just invoke the code and it sends you a response back.
What you can do is you can host all the modules, the code on a virtual machine.
But, you know, if you're hosting the code on a virtual machine, remember that this code doesn't need
to run.
I'm talking about this code, right?
The module code.
It doesn't need to run 24 by seven.
Yes, your web application needs to be running all the time, but these modules only need to run when
they are invoked, right?
So the web application might invoke, let's say one module that will go to an inventory database, fetch
some information and give it back.
That's it.
It only runs for a short duration of time.
Now, if you need to run this code module on a machine, remember the machine then needs to be running
24 by seven.
But your modules are only running you know when they are invoked.
So sometimes it does not make sense to have an entire machine running 24 by seven.
You know, from a costing perspective just to host your modules.
What you can do is you can host your modules on a service known as Azure Functions.
Azure functions is a serverless service.
You again, you don't need to maintain the underlying environment.
You don't maintain the service.
The entire compute environment for running your code is given by the service itself.
All you need to do is to publish your function code onto Azure Functions.
Then your web applications can invoke the functions whenever they are required.
They'll get the output.
Give it back to the web application.
And the best thing is, when it comes down to pricing, you only get charged based on the running time
of the function itself.
So in this chapter, I want to give kind of a quick introduction onto Azure Functions.

--- Primer on containers ---

Mind.
Welcome back.
Now, as part of the exam, we need to have some information when it comes on to using services for
hosting container based applications.
But before that, for the benefit of students, I want to kind of give a quick primer on what exactly
are these container based applications.
A long time ago, when it came on to deploying applications, we had the option of deploying applications
on physical servers.
Then came the advent of virtual machines, where in on the physical server we could install a virtualization
tool.
And then using that virtualization tool, we could host multiple virtual machines, install applications
on these machines.
The virtual machines were designed to make the most out of the hardware specifications of the underlying
physical server.
Well, we could have multiple virtual machines hosted on the same physical server.
But then there were still some problems when it came to deploying applications.
See, to make the most out of each virtual machine, companies would deploy multiple applications on
the same machine.
Let's say that one application required an update at the OS level, the Virtual Machine OS level.
This update could potentially break the other applications running on the same virtual machine.
So this was one common issue.
Another common issue was when applications were being copied from one environment onto another.
So let's say you are an application running in a dev environment.
And for the simplest sake, let's say that we want to transfer that application onto a production based
environment.
The application might be running perfectly on dev, but it would not work on production.
And this could be to the configuration differences at, let's say, the virtual machine level.
In order to kind of alleviate these problems when it came onto configuration differences in the underlying
environment, applications being broken by adding updates, changes, etc., we now have this extra
layer in between that's known as the container engine.
So now you would use let's say a tool set.
A popular tool set is known as Docker.
You can use Docker to kind of containerize your application.
So you would package your application whatever libraries are required by the application itself within
a container.
You then run that container on a virtual machine that has the container runtime installed.
So in this case let's say Docker.
Now here the benefit is each container would only run a single application.
Mostly right.
And it would only contain the libraries that are required to run that application.
That's it.
So each container is kind of running in isolation.
So if you had another application in another container, if that application required changes, you
know, new libraries, new updates, it could only be applied to that container itself and it would
not impact the application running in another container.
So this sort of container based technology was used or is being used to, you know, kind of build these
container based applications.
Once you do build your container based application, in the end we have to run the application itself,
the container based application.
In the next chapter, I want to showcase a very simple example on using a container based service that's
available on the Azure platform.

--- Lab - Azure Container Instance ---

Here I am in my Azure account.
I want to showcase a very simple example on using a container based service.
Obviously, there is a lot that goes into building container based applications and deploying them.
I suggest you go for a dedicated course which focuses on containers, which focuses on using Docker
as a toolset that focuses on using Kubernetes as orchestration tool.
If you're looking at Azure, if you're looking at a certification, then I suggest you go for the AZ
204 based courses.
There you will also learn about using Azure based services when it comes to hosting container based
applications.
But here we want to keep things very, very simple.
In all resources I will click on create.
There are many container based services on offer, but I'm going to use the most easiest one.
And that's known as the container instance service.
So I'll search for container instance.
I'll choose to create a resource based on the service.
And we again get a wizard in which we can create a resource based on the container instance service.
Choose an existing resource group that I have.
I can give a name for the instance in North Europe in terms of the image source.
I'll use the quickstart images.
So what exactly is happening here?
Now in order to kind of host a container based application, first and foremost you need to build your
application.
Next, you need to use Docker as an example, as a toolset for creating something known as an image
out of your application.
Then you host the image in a registry.
You can host the image in, let's say an Azure Container Registry, or host the image in, let's say
Docker Hub.
And then you can run a container out of that image on some sort of compute infrastructure.
So in the end you are running an application.
You need compute machines for running the application, but you're running your application within a
container with the container instance service.
It is providing you the compute infrastructure in the background for running.
Your application has a container.

--- Network Security Groups ---

Welcome back.
Now in this chapter I want to give an introduction onto network security groups.
We have worked a little bit with Nsgs network security groups in the earlier section when we were trying
to give, you know, allow connections onto the web servers running on virtual machines in order to
ensure that requests from our laptop can reach the web servers, we would add an inbound rule onto the
network security group.
Well, let's go into a little bit more detail.
Just what's important from an exam perspective when it comes onto network security groups itself.
So this particular feature is used to filter.
It's like a lightweight firewall.
It filters the traffic between Azure resources in a virtual network.
The network security group actually consists of rules that are used to allow or deny I inbound and outbound
traffic.
You know, I said security is very, very important when it comes on to using services on a cloud platform
on VMs.
Normally, organizations and companies will be hosting softwares, applications.
They want to make sure that they control the connections.
They don't want everybody or all devices to have the ability to connect, because it will be a security
risk.
They don't want to expose, you know, they want to limit the exposure of what clients can actually
probably connect onto the applications running on these machines.
Hence, we can use these network security groups to limit the inbound connections, right?
The inbound traffic and also limit to an extent the outbound connections.
So if there is an outbound connection being made by an application running on a VM, we want to limit
that as well.
When defining rules within an NSG, a network security group, we can define the following.
We can give a name for the rule.
We can give a priority.
So you can have multiple rules within an NSG.
The way that the rules are evaluated are in order of priority.
So you have different priority for the rules.
We can mention a source or a destination.
This can be an IP address, something known as a Cidr block, a service tag, or an application security
group.
We then mention the protocol could be TCP, UDP, ICMP, etc..
What is the port range and what is the action?
Whether it's allow or deny?
It's quite simple in terms of the concept.
In the next chapter let's look at, you know, a little bit on how we can work with the rules in network
security groups.

--- An overview of Network Security Groups ---

Right now in this chapter, let's go through a few points when it comes on to Network Security Group.
So let's go on to our web VM zero one machine.
Right.
We have two machines in place.
We can go on either one.
Each machine by default gets its own network security group.
Now the network security group.
If I go on to a new tab go on to all resources.
So the network security group is a separate resource on its own.
When you go on to web VM zero one.
So we have its own NSG.
This is a separate resource.
We can go on to it and we can go on to settings.
We can define the inbound security rules and we can define the outbound security rules.
Currently this network security group is attached onto the web VM zero one machine, wherein it is attached
onto the network interface that is attached onto the web VM zero one machine itself and energy can be
attached onto either the network interface or onto subnets, so you can attach an NSG onto an entire
subnet.
So instead of maintaining multiple Nsgs for your various machines, you can just have one energy in
place and maintain the rules for your various machines using that one network security group itself.
This is possible.
Now we can define inbound secure rules that defines okay, what are the connections that are inbound
that are allowed.
So we can allow or define.
So we can allow or deny inbound connections onto the virtual machine via the use of inbound security
rules.
And we can allow or deny the outbound connections using outbound security rules.
Now by default when it comes on to an NSG, when it comes onto the inbound security rules, there are
three default rules in place.
So we have something known as an allow VNet inbound.
We have something known as an Allow Azure Load balancer.
And then we have something known as deny all inbound.
Now each particular rule has a name, has a port number a protocol, the source, the destination.
And if you scroll all the way down to the end, let me go ahead and just hide this actually.
So if you scroll on to the end we can see what is the action, whether it's an allow or deny action.
When you establish, you know sorry when a request goes from one device onto another.
So let's say our laptop is sending a request onto the virtual machine.
We want to connect using the Secure Shell protocol.
We want to log in let's say to the Linux based machine.
So our laptop will send a request using the protocol of SSH.
Right.
We're using secure shell.
Secure shell works on the port number of port 22.
Hence here there is a rule.
So this is now a user defined rule.
When we use the wizard Zod to create the Azure VM.
The wizard actually created a rule for us, wherein it added the rule to allow traffic on port 22 of
the machine.
This then allows clients to connect via this port number or onto this port number onto the machine itself.
Here the source is given as any.
That means any client can connect and the destination is any.
That means we can connect onto any destination.
We have the default rules in place as well.
So the first default rule allows communication across all devices within a virtual network.
The next rule actually allows traffic from an Azure load balancer, and the last one denies all other
sort of traffic.
So we can have these rules in place to allow or deny traffic.
Now just to showcase, you know, working with rules, there's a lot that you can do with Nsgs.
In the last chapter from app VM zero one, we were able to send a request using the curl command using
the private IP address.
Now this request is going through because of this default rule.
So we have this default rule in place that allows traffic across the virtual network.
Now when it comes on to the default rules, we can either delete them or we can change them.
But I can add another rule.
Let me add another inbound security rule wherein I put in the source as any in terms of the source,
we can provide either source coming in, you know, the connection coming in from anywhere, or we can
provide saying that, okay, if the connection comes from this particular source IP address, then the
rule should apply to this IP address.
Or we can use a service tag.
So there are different service tags in place.
We can say that if requests are coming in from the internet, then this rule should apply to those requests.
If requests are coming in from another Azure based service, this rule should apply to that particular
service.
So we have different sort of, you know, basically sources in place.
And then we have something known as an application security group.
Let me leave it as any in terms of the service.
What I'll do is I'll put star.
So I'll say that let's go ahead and make sure that this rule applies to all types of traffic.
You know, any protocol.
And in the action I'm going to put deny this basically is saying that we want to deny all traffic.
And then we have a priority.
So here it's giving a priority of 310.
Let me go ahead and click on add.
So now we're adding a security rule to deny all inbound traffic or to the machine.
So this rule is coming up second in the list.
Now since we have the first rule which is to allow traffic on SSH, we should still be able to connect
onto the machine.
So when we connect onto the machine the energy will first evaluate this rule.
This rule says that please allow traffic.
So in the end, whatever rule it encounters first, whichever rule matches the request that will be
considered and it will ignore the other rules.
Hence, we should be able to successfully connect to the machine.
We should be able to successfully connect onto web VM zero one.
But now what about sending a request again using the curl command?

reached.

groups.

--- Application Security Groups ---

Now, in this chapter, I want to give kind of a quick note on something known as application security
groups.
Now, application security groups is nothing but an extension onto network security groups itself.
You know here what you can do is you can group virtual machines when it comes down to defining the required
security policies.
And what do I mean by this?
So in a company, let's say that they deploy multiple machines within a virtual network.
So let's say that they have the web layer of their application hosted on a set of web based machines.
Then they have the application layer set up on another set of machines.
So these are the application servers the web servers.
Normally you have different tiers for the application.
And then finally they might host the databases on a set of database servers.
So now you know when they have these machines in place they want to define rules in the network secure
group in such a way that they want to say that okay.
Requests from the internet can, first of all, flow onto the web servers, then incoming traffic onto
the application servers should only come in from the web server.
So the web servers should communicate with the application servers only.
And then the database servers should only accept requests from the application servers.
So they have this kind of flow when it comes on to this multi-tier application.
Now when you start having multiple servers in the network security group, you would have a lot of complications
because you would probably specify the individual IP address of each and every machine in the rules.
So when you go for further exams, further courses on understanding more of the networking aspect,
you'll understand that when you start having now multiple machines in the network, When you start defining
rules in the NSG, you can get a lot of rules in place and it can become a maintenance overhead in trying
to maintain so many rules, because you will have rules of just pinpointing each and every IP address
and making sure the communication is only possible between a set of machines.
To alleviate this, what you can do is you can define something known as application security groups.
You can define just one group of web servers.
And then you can kind of logically link this on to these web servers.
You can have another application security group and only link it onto the application servers.
So it's like you're just tagging those servers in one group.
You're saying, okay, we have web servers in another group, we have application servers, and in another
group we have database servers.
Then in the network security group itself, you can just simply have a rule saying that, okay, requests
from the internet can flow onto the application security group, which in turn means that requests can
go in onto the web servers.
Then we can have another rule saying that, okay, only traffic from the web server application security
group can flow into the application security group for the application servers.
That's it.
We can just have one rule between these two asgs.
That's it.
We don't have to have rules for each and every server, because in the end, when we allow the rules
between the asgs, it just means that internally, yes, the traffic is allowed between the servers
from the web layer and the application layer.
So it is meant to simplify the rules that you define in your network security groups.
So in this chapter I want to give this kind of quick note when it comes to application security groups.

In your virtual network.

This is a separate resource.

--- Overview of Azure ExpressRoute ---

Now, in this chapter, I want to give kind of a brief overview on Azure Expressroute.
In the prior chapter, I talked about having these VPN connections in place where you can have either
a point to site VPN connection.
So if you have individual clients that need to connect onto resources within a virtual network, at
that point in time, you can create point to site VPN connections.
If you need an entire site, an entire network in your on premises data center to connect onto the virtual
network, then that time you can create a site to site VPN connection.
All of these connections are established over the internet.
Yes, you do have a secure and encrypted connection between your clients, your sites, and Azure,
but sometimes companies want to have kind of a private pipe, you know, a private connection between
their on premises network and Azure.
Now for this, what they can do is they can make use of Azure Expressroute.
With Azure Expressroute you get something known as Expressroute circuits.
These are connections can be established using your internet services providers.
Or your internet services provider should be having the capability of having a private connection from
their endpoint, you know, from from their site on to Microsoft.
And what you will do is you can make use of internet services providers to establish the sort of private
connection.
So it's not a cheap connection.
It's expensive.
But then what happens is that you get a private, dedicated connection with a particular speed.
So instead of depending upon the internet, right, you know, when you are working with resources over
the internet.
Obviously it depends on your internet bandwidth speed, but with a dedicated connection, you have different
speeds in place.
You can get higher speeds.
That means if you need better access to your resources in Microsoft, you can use an expressroute circuit.
And I said here, the best thing is that it's private in nature.
The communication between your clients and Microsoft does not go over the public internet.
It's a private connection.
You can use these expressroute circuits to establish connections to your resources.
Working in a virtual network.
At the same time, you can also connect on to public offerings from Microsoft.
So for example, you can establish or you can work with public resources like Azure SQL databases or
maybe Microsoft 365.
All of this via a private Expressroute connection.
You can actually go to the documentation page to understand more about Azure Expressroute.
So in this chapter, I want to give kind of this brief overview on, you know, this brief understanding
on this particular service.

--- Azure Load Balancer ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now we're going to be looking at a service known as the Azure Load Balancer Service.
First I want to give an introduction onto the purpose of the service altogether.
Now we have this concept of a load balancer.
So forget Azure.
There is a concept.
There is something that you can actually deploy known as a load balancer.
The entire purpose of a load balancer is to distribute incoming requests from clients on to your back
end.
Resources.
In an earlier section, you know, we had seen how we could deploy multiple virtual machines onto a
network.
We had also seen something, you know, a concept known as a virtual machine scale set.
Now, if you have multiple VMs that is hosting the same application and you want the user load, so
you want the user traffic to be distributed across these machines, right?
You want the load to be distributed across multiple virtual machines.
Multiple compute machines.
You need something in the front that will take in the request and distribute the requests across the
different machines.
For this, you can make use of a load balancer, so you can install a load balancing service in front
of your machines.
The Azure Load Balancer is a managed service wherein you don't need to install any load balancer solution.
You can make use of the service to load balance your requests, your incoming network traffic across
your backend servers.
So you can have back end servers running as part of a virtual network.
So let's say you have a web application running on a set of machines.
You can use the load balancing service to distribute the incoming requests.
Now the load balancer actually distribute the requests across the private IP addresses of the backend
machines.
So as such, we don't need public IP addresses to be assigned on the backend machines itself.
Now just a quick note on the various components of we will go through chapters on how do we provision
and make use of the Azure Load Balancing service.
But before that, just a quick note on some of the components of the load balancing service.
First is the frontend IP configuration.
So you know, what we can do is I told you that we don't need to have a public IP address assigned onto
the machines, but if you want requests from the internet to come onto the load balancing solution,
we will need a public IP address assigned onto the load balancer.
So instead of having a public IP address assigned onto each individual VM, we have a public IP address
assigned onto the load balancer.
The request will come onto the load balancer, and the load balancer can direct the request via the
private IP addresses.
So for this, for the public IP address configuration of the load balancing solution, we can make use
of a frontend IP configuration.
Then we have the backend pool.
So the backend pool will contain your machines right.
We need to configure what are the machines that will sit behind the load balancer?
We have something known as a health probe.
See the load balancer distributes requests across your backend machines.
But suppose if one of the machines is not working, it's down.
The load balancing solution should understand that the machine is not available and hence not send the
client request there.
But you have to do something to make sure that the machine comes back up.
But in the meantime, the load balancing solution should understand that a machine is down.
Do not send the client request onto that machine.
So for this you can make use of a health probe.
The health probe can be used by the load balancing solution to understand at regular intervals whether
the machine is in a healthy state.
Then we can define load balancing rules.
These are used to determine the request from clients and how they are routed onto the backend.
Virtual machines.
Right.
So just a quick introduction.
Let's move on to the subsequent chapters where we'll look at how can we use the load balancing solutions.

--- Introduction to Azure Storage Accounts ---

Hello and welcome on to this section.
In this section we are going to cover an Azure storage option that's known as Azure Storage Accounts.
In this chapter, I want to give a quick introduction to Azure Storage Account.
Service is a storage on Azure that can be used to.
It can be used as a mechanism for storage of blob objects, files, queues, and tables.
There are different types of storage accounts.
We are going to be focusing on something known as standard general purpose V2 storage accounts.
This account gives us access to the following services.
So we get access to the blob service, the file Share service, the Queue service and the table service.
When we do create a resource based on the service of the Azure Storage account, an important thing
is that the name of the resource needs to be unique within Azure.
So I said, we get access to full services.
Let me go through a quick overview of these four services.
First is the blob service.
The blob service is used to store large amounts of unstructured data.
This is suitable for storing images, documents, videos, and audio files.
So for example, in your storage account, we can make use of the blob storage service to store files
such as your video files, your audio files.
The first step is within the blob service.
You create something known as a storage container.
The storage container is used to organize your blob based objects.
Don't worry, we'll be going through chapters on how to use these various services.
First and foremost, I want to give a quick introduction onto these services.
Then we have Azure files.
Now let's say in a company users need to access their own respective file shares.
So you want to give their you know, you want to give some locations to users so that you can store
files.
In order to make this work, you will first need to deploy a file server on which they can store their
files.

You are responsible for making sure that storage is sufficient so that users can store their files in
the shared folders.
Well, instead of using or maintaining your own file shares, what you can do is you can make use of
the file share service within Azure Storage accounts.
So in the storage account, you can just go ahead and create different file shares for your users.
These are file shares are fully managed.
They can be accessed via the normal protocols of Server Message Block and the Network File System Protocol.
And here the physical storage is managed for you.
Then we have the very simple queue service.
This is a messaging service.

--- Azure Storage Accounts - Blob Access tiers ---

Now I want to go through a concept known as access tiers.
When it comes onto your blog based objects in Azure storage accounts.
Now for us, what we've been doing is we've been storing a very few files.
Right.
Just a couple of files within a container in Azure Storage account.
But normally a company might store millions of objects within an Azure storage account.
You know, on Azure and Azure Storage account can actually double down on something known as a Data
Lake Gen2 storage account when the company uses the storage account has a data lake.
They ingest all sorts all types of data within the storage account itself so they can have multiple
storage containers.
Multiple, let's say log files coming in has objects.
They are ingesting this onto the blob service within a storage account because it's setting it up as
a data lake.
So companies might have millions of objects within the blob service within an Azure Storage account.
Now, there are various factors that govern the pricing when it comes onto Azure storage.
One important aspect is the storage itself.
Here I'm just giving a snippet from the Microsoft documentation when it comes onto the pricing for something
known as the different access tiers.
You know, when it comes on to an object, by default, the objects are set onto the hot access tier.
Here we can see that when it comes onto the first 50TB of data per month, there is a cost per gig for
the next 450TB per month.
Again, there's another cost linked to it.
So one of the important factors when it comes on to costing for Azure storage is the storage itself,
the storage cost.
Now for us, it doesn't make that much of a difference because we just have a few objects in place.
Why is it when companies store millions and millions of objects within Azure Storage.
Then this does make a difference.
And it's always the case that, you know, initially a company might use objects quite frequently,
but then after some time they don't need the objects anymore.
I mean, they don't need to access the objects.
They probably still need the objects to be in place, but they don't need to access the objects because
it.
Let's say that in this use case, the company is ingesting, let's say, log files from an application
and storing it as objects in a container in a storage account.
Then you have another application that is processing those files.
Once the application has processed those files, those files are not required to be processed anymore
or not required to be accessed anymore.
Yes, the company still needs to retain the files, but at the moment they don't need to.
You know, they don't need that aspect of being required to view or process the files any further.
So could be that, you know, every day a batch of files is coming in, the files need to be recovered,
processed and then maybe stored in another container and just kept over there.
They don't need to be processed anymore.
No one needs to read the files, but they still need to be in place.
Now, when you start ingesting files on a daily basis and you have processed them, you want to save
on costs when it comes onto the process files.
Well, what you can do is you can change the access tier of those process files.
There are different access tiers when we upload our objects.
I told you by default they go on to the hot access tier.
This is the default tier for objects.
This is optimized for objects that are accessed frequently.
Then we have the cool access tier.
Now the cool access tier is ideal for objects that are infrequently accessed, so I said it's good for
those files after they've been processed.
You convert the access tier of those files to the cool access tier, but here you have to make sure
that the files are stored for a minimum of at least 30 days.
Here the storage costs are lower, but then the access costs are higher.
So what do I mean by this?
So if you scroll on top again just to compare these storage costs, what you will see here is when it
comes onto the storage costs for objects in the cool access tier, you will see that it's a little bit
less than that for the objects in the hot access tier.
And when you have millions of objects in place, this does make a big difference.
But what I'm saying here is two important aspects.
One is that, uh, if I scroll down.
So one is that the storage costs are lower.
That's what I'm saying here when compared with the hot access tier.
But the access costs are higher.
I told you, when it comes onto Azure storage, there are different parameters that need to be considered
when it comes onto costing.
One parameter is the storage cost itself, but another parameter is the access cost.
So there is a small charge that is incurred when you, for example, access an object.
So there's not only a charge for storing the object, there's also a cost for accessing the object.
So when you have objects in the cool access tier, yes the storage costs are less.
But then when you start accessing the object, it's more it's more than that of the objects in the hot
access tier.
That's why the cool access tier is ideal for objects that are infrequently accessed.
Second, they need to be stored for a minimum of at least 30 days.
For example, let's say you store the objects in the hot or in the cool access tier for around ten days,
but then you decide, okay, you need to convert them back onto the hot access tier, which you can
do, but you need to convert them back because they are being accessed more frequently and you are incurring
higher access costs.
Well, the problem is you will still be charged for the 30 days.
So I said there's a minimum of 30 days that needs to be in place for the objects in the cool access
tier.
So if you change the tier before 30 days again, you will still be charged as a charge for that 30 days.
So you need to be very careful, you understand?
Okay.
Really, are the objects not being accessed that frequently?
Then you can convert the tier to the cool access tier.
Then we have the cold access tier.
This is ideal for objects that are rarely accessed or modified, but you still need to access them.
An object can be set on the cool access tier.
Here, the object needs to be stored for a minimum of 90 days.
And then finally we have the archive access tier.
This is ideal for objects that are rarely accessed, but if you need to access them, there is a waiting
period.
So it's not that you can access the object directly because see here in the archive access tier, it's
the least cost for storage.
But when you want to access the file, you first have to send a request.
Then the storage service will kind of change the tier of that object.
And then you can access the object.
So there's a caveat here when it comes to storing objects in the archive access tier.
And here the data need to be stored for at least 180 days a minimum of 180 days.

in Azure storage.

--- Azure Storage Accounts - Data Redundancy ---

Now, in this chapter, I want to go through the data redundancy concept when it comes onto Azure Storage.
Now, when it comes down to services that Azure offers, they make sure that they maintain high availability.
So when it comes onto Azure storage, they maintain high availability of their data.
They guarantee an uptime percentage of more than 99%, 99.9%.
And in case if they breach that service, they will actually give you service credit.
So we are talking about if you look at the case of Azure Storage, we are talking about storing our
data.
So we are trusting that Azure has the right storage in place, the right capabilities of keeping our
data not only safe, but having, you know, high durability, making sure that we don't lose our data.
Hence, we have that trust with Azure that they will keep our data safe.
So when it comes to data within Azure Storage, they have these different data redundancy options in
place.
And by data redundancy I mean that they will make sure that we don't lose our data.
Now they have different data redundancy options in place.
By default we have locally redundant storage.
This is the least redundant storage option in locally redundant storage.
Azure makes three copies of your data within a single data center.
So when you upload a file as an object in the blob service in your Azure storage account, there are
three copies of your data made within a single data center.
So if there is any problem, if one object gets corrupted and it's not available, your object has such
will still be available because there are multiple copies in place.
So even with the least redundant storage option, there are still three copies of your data made within
a single data center.
Then we have the next redundancy option that's known as zone redundant storage.
Now we talked about the concept of Azure regions having something known as availability zones.
So within a region you will have multiple availability zones.
Each availability zone is a collection of data centers.
So in locally redundant storage what happens if the entire data center goes down?
Then your objects are not available with the help of zone redundant storage your objects.
Your data is replicated across the three availability zones in a particular region.
So now you have more data redundancy in place.
Even if a data center goes down, your data will still be available because it will be available in
another data center, in another availability zone.
But we could go a step further.
Let's say that the entire region goes down.
So let's say in this case you have created an Azure storage account in the east US location.
The entire region has gone down and you can't access your objects.
Now please note that there's quite a big event if a region is not available with companies that really
store sensitive and critical data.
They might have a requirement wherein they cannot, even for a few minutes, have their data not available.
So they would need to look at other options, right.
More than just zone redundant storage.
So we have now geo redundant storage.
So what is geo redundant storage?
In this particular redundancy option, three copies of your data are made to a single physical location
in the primary region using locally redundant storage.
So here when you upload an object again three copies are made to the data center to one data center
in the primary region.
So let's say east us.
If your storage account is in east US then with Azure you have something known as no paired regions.
So with East US West US is taken as a paired region.
So let's say that you've deployed your storage account onto the east US location.
What will happen is in geo redundant storage, your data is replicated to another region altogether.
So if the primary region goes down the main region for hosting your storage account, you can still
access your data in the secondary region.
And obviously it's great that we have these various options of increasing our data redundancy.
But what we have to understand is that the cost is also going to increase for each increase redundancy
option.
So here you are going to pay double the cost of storing objects in the primary and in the secondary
location.
At the same time you also pay for the bandwidth cost for the data being transferred from primary to
secondary.
So it's only for those companies that really, really need a high redundancy option for their data that
they should opt for this.
And then we have a variation when it comes onto geo redundant storage.
And that's known as geo zone redundant storage.
So in geo redundant storage in the primary location okay your data is stored using LRS.
All the objects are in one data center but in geo zone redundant storage in the primary location.
Your data is replicated not just in a data center, but across three availability zones in the primary
region.
And again, three copies in your data is copied onto a secondary location.
So we have these different redundancy options in place when it comes onto your storage accounts.
So in Azure when you create a storage account.
So let's say I'm going through the process of creating a storage account here.
Let me just choose an existing resource group.
And you can give a storage account name.
We can choose the primary service.
We can choose the performance.
We have the redundancy options in place.
So we have the geo redundant storage.
We have the locally redundant storage.
We have zone redundant and we have geo zone redundant.
So when we create our storage account we have the option of choosing our redundancy for our objects
within the storage account itself.
Right.
So in this chapter I want to go through the concept when it comes onto redundancy.

The entire purpose of Azure files is you can create file shares.
You can give it give it to your users.
And this alleviates the burden of the company need to manage and maintain storage and systems required
to create and maintain these shares.

that need to access files, they need quick access to certain set of files.
So yes, we can go ahead and use Azure Files as a managed service for storing our files.
But there are cases wherein I said applications and users need fast access to files.
So even though we can quickly get files from Azure file shares, sometimes even a small fraction of
latency, you know, when it comes to applications is a problem.
It can cause an issue.
You know, what you can do is you can make use of the file sync service to convert a Windows server.
So what you can do is on your local data center, you can create a Windows Server, have a Windows Server
in place.
This can behave as a local cache for the commonly accessed files on this machine.
On the server, you will install an agent known as Azure File Sync.
That Azure File Sync agent can sync all of the commonly used files from your Azure file shares onto
your local data center.
You know, it's more it's then more locally available to your users and applications.
So then your users and applications can then fetch these common use files directly from the Windows
Server itself, rather than the request going onto the file share.
So yes, the file shares will still be there.
I said it's only for those commonly required files that you can, you know, the users and applications
can make a request and the files will be recovered or the files will be taken from the local Windows
server.
This local Windows Server, I said, will have an agent running that's known as the Azure File Sync
Service.

for your file shares.

difficult.
You're going to get broken connections.
You won't be able to send all of the files.
It will be a lot of issues Use to send your data in a quick, inexpensive and reliable way.

You will actually make an order for a data box device.
Then Microsoft will actually send you.
If I just scroll down, they will send you an entire data box, right.

you want to copy onto Azure onto the data box.
Send the data box back to Microsoft.
And then the data engineers at Microsoft would copy the data onto your Azure account.

onto Azure.

platform.

Now you might be familiar with the concept of a relational database system.
Right.
We have some famous ones.
We have the popular relational database management systems of Oracle.
We have MySQL.
We have Microsoft SQL server.
What do you normally do is you would install either engine.
So for example you could install Microsoft SQL server as a database engine on a machine.
So let's say a VM and then you host a database.
You have tables in place.
For example, if you have an e-commerce application hosted on another system, the application could
access the database system, access the information within the tables.
You could have a table that has information about the various products that the e-commerce application
is selling, various orders being placed on the site, information about customers, so on and so forth.
So you could have this kind of setup in place wherein you have a database hosted on one compute machine,
and you have the application hosted on another machine.
When it comes on to the database system.
So you need to first of all download and install the database engine on a machine, then configure the
database engine, host a database, and then have the information right in the form of tables.
So from a database admin perspective we have some responsibilities, you know, wherein you need to
manage the uptime of the database server.
You would need to have database backups and restoration processes in place.
You would need to install patches at regular intervals, both at the OS level and the database engine
level.
Companies might not want the burden of actually managing the entire database system.
They would probably want to have a manage system in place a managed service.
Well, for this we can make use of the Azure SQL Database Service.
Here, the underlying database server and the database system is actually managed by the service itself.
So the service will provide the compute infrastructure like the virtual machine, whatever that will
be hosting the actual database engine.
What you can just do is you can just start working with the databases itself and hosting your tables
of information here.
The underlying server is managed for you.
The database software will be in place.
There are also features wherein it has automatic backup and restoration process in place, and there
are various other features.
So if you want to have a managed platform, a managed service in place wherein you have, you know,
you need to manage the underlying database infrastructure, we can make use of the Azure SQL Database
Service.
Now let's move on to the next chapter.
Let's look at an example on using this particular service.

--- About Azure Cosmos DB ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now in this chapter, I want to give a brief introduction onto the next service that we're going to
be seeing when it comes on to databases.
And that's Azure Cosmos DB.
Now Azure Cosmos DB primarily the engine is based on NoSQL, but we have other APIs in place as well.
So first and foremost, I want to give a quick introduction on what exactly is NoSQL.
We're all familiar with relational database systems.
We have seen an example of the Azure SQL database, which is a cloud managed platform for Microsoft
SQL server.
When it comes on to a relational database system, normally you would have tables in place.
These tables have a schema in which you define the column names.
You define the column types.
You would probably have relationships between tables within a database engine.
Hence the entire purpose of having a relational database system in place.
So primarily, you know, in the past you would have applications that would only work with relational
database systems.
Over time, they came requirements wherein applications didn't need to have or work with relational
data.
They just want to have the ability to store large amounts of data.
This did not need to be based on transactions.
Just have the ability to store and access large amounts of data.
No relationships required and no kind of predefined schema.
You know, in today's world, you have data that's probably coming in from a variety of systems.
And this data comes in in all shapes and sizes.
Nothing is fixed.
There's no structure.
So I said over time, you know, there was a requirement wherein you need to have kind of database engines
that could work with data that's flexible in nature, that does not need to conform to any sort of schema.
And also you don't need to have any sort of relationships between the tables itself, between the data.
So for this, you know, we have the advent of NoSQL databases.
So there are some familiar ones.
There are some famous ones.
We have MongoDB that was used or that can be used for, you know, basically storing documents within
a database, not necessarily in the form of tables.
And it's now this, you know, NoSQL databases that are very popular nowadays and are being used by
applications when it comes to data storage and data retrieval.
Now we come on to Azure Cosmos DB.
So Azure Cosmos DB is a fully managed NoSQL relational and vector database.
So there's a lot that has been added initially when it came onto Azure Cosmos DB the focus was NoSQL,
but there is a lot of functionality that's been added onto the service itself.
So if you wanted to have a NoSQL data store similar to a database engine, what you need to do is you
need to have, first of all, a compute machine in place on that machine, install the NoSQL engine.
So for example, if you're using MongoDB, you would need to download the MongoDB software installed
on a machine, configure it, and then maintain that infrastructure with Azure Cosmos DB.
Again, the entire infrastructure, the physical, the virtual infrastructure is managed for you.
You directly start storing data and working with the data.
You get also fast access to your data.
That is also another important concept.
Now with Azure Cosmos DB.
The other thing is when you create a cosmos DB account, you can choose from a variety of APIs if you
want the traditional NoSQL SQL data store.
You can use the no SQL API.
Here the data is stored in document format and you can query for items using the very familiar Structured
Query Language.
If you already have an existing MongoDB database in place and you want to migrate onto Azure, you can
make use of Azure Cosmos DB for MongoDB.
So when you create your Azure Cosmos DB account, you can decide what API to use for the underlying
account.
Based on the API, you can store your data accordingly.
You also have an API for Postgres SQL, which is another popular relational database system.
And we have support for Apache Cassandra.
We have Gremlin, which is a graph based database, and we also have the table API, which is quite
similar to the table service that we have within Azure Storage accounts.
So first and foremost I want to give a quick introduction onto Azure Cosmos DB.
