--- Enterprise Data Engineering ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now, in the prior set of chapters, we had looked at some of the database engines that are available
on Azure.
In the next set of chapters, I want to go through some of these services that can be used from a data
engineering perspective.
First and foremost, I want to give a note on some of the important concepts when it comes onto the
entire, you know, what services or what tools do you require when it comes on to the entire data engineering
process itself?
So first is building a data lake.
What you can do is, you know, in today's world, we have data coming in from a variety of data sources.
We have data coming in at a high rate.
We have data coming in in different types, having different schemas.
What we can do is we can take all of that data.
So whether it be structured data, whether it be semi-structured data, whether it be unstructured data,
and we can store all of that data within a data lake a huge expanse.
Now from here we can take in that raw data, we can transform that data.
Or if you have structured data that's coming in, you can use tools to kind of create reports, perform
data science, perform machine learning, so on and so forth.
So first is the data lake.
The next thing is the entire ETL process.
So when you have data coming in onto your data lake, if you have raw data, what you'll want to do
is you will want to go ahead and extract data that's relevant.
You would want to perform some sort of transformation because the data is in raw format.
So you might want to only select certain types of data, certain amounts of data.
You would want to restructure the data.
You might want to filter the data clean the data.
You want to enrich the data in such a way that it adds business value.
So in the end, businesses want to take in the data and add value.
They want to get reports they want to perform analysis on the data.
So the entire ETL process is built around extracting data, transforming the data, and then loading
it onto a data destination.
You can load the data onto a data warehouse.
A data warehouse is nothing.
You know, it's like a database system.
It's based on SQL.
And again, your data is based in the form of tables.
So you have a SQL engine that drives the querying of data from a data warehouse.
But within a data warehouse the focus is more on gaining insights from the data.
If you look at a traditional relational database system, that database system is used more from a transactional
aspect.
You have applications that are reading and writing data onto a database system.
But from a data warehouse perspective, what you do is you feed in historical data onto the data warehouse,
and then you start extracting insights based on the historical data you query for data, you build reports
out of the data.
So you take the ETL process, you extract the raw information, you transform it, and you can place
it within a data warehouse.
Then we have the concept of a data lake house.
The data Lake house is is put into place to provide the benefits of a data warehouse.
The benefits of a data lake.
The problems with the data lake is because you have so much data coming in from different streams,
different types, different schemas.
Governance becomes a headache in terms of governing the data with a data warehouse.
It's not cheap.
So database systems are very expensive if you want to host a data warehouse.
So what you can do is, you know, nowadays companies have this concept of a data lake House, which
is meant to give you the best of both worlds, give you the best of, you know, having a data warehouse
in place and a data lake now within Azure.
What you can do is you can use Azure Data Lake Gen two storage accounts when it comes onto your data
lake.
You can use tools such as a stream ad job.
You can use pipelines in Azure Data Factory or Azure Synapse to take in your streaming data and place
it in a data lake.
You can use services like Azure Databricks.
You can use the Apache Spark engine within Azure Databricks or within Azure Synapse to take in and transform
your data.
And what else can we do?
We can then serve our data, you know, take our data and serve it.
Why a SQL data warehouse.
But you can do Azure Synapse and build reports.
We now also have Microsoft Fabric, which is a software as a solution in which you can ingest your data.
You have the use of pipelines, you can build data warehouses, you can build lakehouses, you can use
power BI.
It's kind of a one stop shop for, you know, all of your data engineering needs.

--- Benefits of the cloud - High Availability ---

So now in this section, I want to go through some of the important cloud concepts based on the objectives
of the exam.
The first thing I want to cover is this concept of high availability.
So you will hear of terms like high availability, fault tolerance, elasticity, scalability, so on
and so forth.
What is high availability.
So this is ensuring that the application that you deploy is made available with the minimum amount of
downtime based on various issues.
So let's consider a very simple use case scenario wherein you have a web application hosted on an Azure
VM.
Right.
Users are accessing the web application, and this application is accessing an Azure SQL database as
a backend data store.
Now what happens if the web application goes down?
Well, your end users are not happy.
Your operations team can get flooded with calls from users, and the entire business loses confidence
in the company if that company is responsible for maintaining the application.
Now, there are so many reasons as to why the application could go down.
A very simple case is maybe an update.
You know, a newer version was published for the application code.
Now, because of that new update, it caused some issue in the application itself, right?
It could cause a memory leak in the act.
So it could cause a performance issue or a memory leak in the application.
And then because of that extended usage, the application goes down.
Or maybe an update, a security patch was applied at the OS level on the virtual machine level.
Now because of that patch update, it caused an issue.
It can, you know, it cause an issue in a library being used by the application, an application library.
So again the application goes down.
So this is from the application side.
From the Azure site.
Maybe the physical server hosting the virtual machine ran into technical issues and had to be restarted.
There was an issue for the overall Azure SQL database service in the region the database is hosted in.
So you have various issues that can arise when it comes on to an application going down.
When you're looking at high availability, you're looking at implementing high availability at the infrastructure
level.
We've already seen from Azure VMs how we can avoid this, how we can avoid our infrastructure going
down.
Here are.
The best case is to make sure that you implement your web application across a set of virtual machines,
and if you're implementing your application across a set of virtual machines, you can ensure that those
virtual machines are made part of availability sets or they are across multiple availability zones.
So here, if a physical server had to go down in terms of an availability set, your application would
still be available.
If an entire data center were to go down and you still want to make your application available, you
want to make your infrastructure highly available.
Then you would distribute your infrastructure, your virtual machines across various availability zones.
Obviously, at an application level, you can implement procedures to roll back onto a prior working
application version.
And when it comes onto the Azure SQL database, you also have a number of options in place from the
service itself.
So the service itself is actually built already for high availability.
But they do have other options in place here for each and every part of your application landscape.
You have to see where the application can go down, and I need to see how you can add measures to your
application landscape to increase its availability.
So in this chapter, I quickly want to go through the concept of high availability.

--- Benefits of the cloud - Disaster recovery ---

Now, in this chapter, I briefly want to go through the concept of disaster recovery.
This refers to the ability to recover from either a service failure or a region failure as an example.
So let's say that you are making use of the Azure VM service and storage accounts, and you're hosting
these or making use of these resources in the East US location.
Now let's say that the entire east location goes down.
So you don't have any infrastructure in place to support these applications or to support your data.
What you can do is you can make sure that your infrastructure, when it comes onto your virtual machines
and your storage accounts, are available in another location.
There are services on Azure that can constantly replicate the data from the primary region onto the
secondary.
So in case if the primary region goes down, you can quickly spin up your or have your infrastructure
ready and in place and with the most recent data right in another location.
So there are these tools in place.
But what we have to remember is from a costing perspective, right.
There is an increase in cost because then you need to maintain your infrastructure in both the primary
and the secondary location.
In this chapter, briefly want to go to the concept when it comes onto disaster recovery.

--- Cloud service model ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now in this chapter, I briefly want to go through the aspects when it comes on to cloud service model.
Now we have seen how to use services like Azure Virtual Machine Service, how we could use the Azure
SQL database Service, how we could use let's say Azure Cosmos DB.
Now in all of these services, there are some aspects that you have to maintain.
There are certain aspects that you just could get started using the service.
So for example we came onto Azure Cosmos DB.
Then you don't need to maintain the physical or the virtual infrastructure.
You can directly go onto the interface the data explorer, create your database, create your container
and start adding your data.
These are services come under these you know overarching cloud service models.
So we have infrastructure as a service.
We have platform as a service.
And we have software as a service to kind of distinguish between these different models.
We have these different layers.
So if you're looking at the entire, you know, architecture of an application, where in you have the
application itself, you have the data, right?
The application is using if the application is based, let's say on dotnet, so you have the runtime
for the application itself, you have the OS itself.
You have then at the physical layer you have the virtualization, the physical servers, the physical
storage and the physical networking.
So all of these are the different layers when it comes on to hosting an application and having data
in place.
When it comes on to infrastructure as a service, the cloud provider is responsible for the physical
hardware.
So the virtualization, the servers, the storage, networking, so on and so forth.
As a customer, we are responsible for like for example, Azure VMs is an example of infrastructure
as a service.
So there we provision the virtual machine.
We decide on the OS for the machine itself.
We are responsible for patching the OS, you know, applying these security patches at the OS level.
So Microsoft will ensure that patching is done for the physical servers, the security patches.
But at the virtual machine level, it is our responsibility to ensure that we patch we install the main
security patches at the OS level.
We are then responsible for hosting the application, installing the required runtime, installing the
application, and then storing the data.
And this is from the broad level when it comes on to infrastructure as a service then we have platform
as a service.
Now in platform as a service, the cloud provider is now they have the additional responsibility of
maintaining even the virtual infrastructure.
So they would maintain the operating system.
They would maintain the patching on a regular basis for the OS itself.
They also are responsible for the runtime.
So as an example, if you look at the Azure SQL Database Service.
This is an example of a platform as a service.
There you saw that in addition to the physical infrastructure, even the compute infrastructure is managed
by Azure.
All you have to do is to start working with the database itself.
That's it.
You start working with the data.
The underlying database engine is installed and configured for you.
The patching, even at the database level, is managed by the service itself.
And then finally we have software as a service where everything is managed basically by the vendor.
You just start basically using the application.
That's it.
An example is Microsoft 365.
So if we look at using let's say office or even using email right.
In the end all you have to do is just use the application.
Right.
And the data.
That's it.
You are not responsible for installing or configuring the application, maintaining the infrastructure.
Nothing.
Everything is done by the cloud provider itself.
So in this chapter briefly we want to go through the cloud service model.

--- Cloud model types ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now in this chapter I want to go through the different cloud model types.
The first one is what we're working with.
We are working with Azure.
We are working with a public cloud service.
This is a platform that's available over the internet.
So your customers can use a portion of the cloud when it comes onto their hosting requirements with
Azure.
We've seen that we have you know, we don't need to have any capital expenditure in place.
So there's a reduction in capital investment.
We have a pay as you go pricing model wherein we pay based on how much we use.
We have the ability to scale on demand whenever required, and we can always make use of the latest
services that Azure has to offer.
So these are some of the key benefits when it comes on to using a public cloud platform.
Next we have the concept of a private cloud.
Now within a private cloud, a company might set up kind of a cloud environment within their own data
center, or they might use another data center that's being managed by another provider.
But in the end, it's kind of a private data center.
It's only for the company itself.
You're again, they have services that are made available for them.
the benefits are that the company can implement their own security protocols.
They own and manage the entire infrastructure, and these services are only available for the company
users.
The downsides are since they now manage the physical infrastructure, if they do need to scale the infrastructure,
they would need to buy physical hardware.
And the next is difficult to cover.
You know, all of the new services offered by public cloud providers.
So it's not so simple.
If you have a new kind of software that you need to implement, it's not so easy to provide as a service.
And then next we have kind of a hybrid setup wherein you can make the best of both worlds.
So a company could use the public cloud offering for hosting a set of applications.
And they could host another set of applications on their private cloud network.
So here you get the best of both worlds.
You can have security in place.
You know, the right security protocols for certain applications in the private cloud.
And when you want to scale based on demand, when you want to make use of public cloud platforms, you
can make use of, let's say, Azure as a cloud platform.
Well, in this chapter, I just want to go through something known as the cloud model types.

--- Economies of scale ---

and I'm terming this under the chapter
of economics of scale.
So if you look at the basics,
the economies of scale relates to the ability
to carry out tasks more efficiently
or at a lower-cost per unit when operating at a large scale.
So if you look at a cloud platform,
they have so many services,
they have so much physical hardware,
there's so much infrastructure,
and at the same time, obviously,
they are getting more and more customers onboard
to use their services.
So when you start getting more and more customers
to actually use services on the cloud,
when you get more customers
to use the physical hardware, because in the end,
in the Azure data centers,
Microsoft needs to make that initial investment
to buy the physical hardware,
the storage, the networking, maintain the data centers.
They're doing everything themselves, right?
And they're providing a service to you.
So when the demand, when the customer demand increases,
cloud providers can then get hardware at discounted prices.
This becomes a benefit then in the end to the customer,
wherein the discounts they get
can be passed onto the customer itself.
So if the number of customers increases,
then there can be significant changes to the service costs.
The service costs can actually go down.
Now, some other concepts.
One is the concept of having capital expenditure in place.
Your money is paid upfront.
For example, I said, let's say that a company,
you know, does not use a cloud
and they wanna host applications
in their on-premises data centers.
So initially, they would need to pay,
they would need to invest money
to buy service for hosting the application.
They would need to invest in storage, right,
for storage of data, so this involves storage costs.
If they need to host,
you know, some third party vendor applications,
some softwares, they would need to purchase licenses.
Sometimes these also come under capital expenditure.
And then you have the data center costs, you know,
you have various aspects that can add up
to the entire capital expenditure for the company itself.
And then we have the operational expenditure.
This is the ongoing money spent on expenses.
So for example, the human resources that are required to,
let's say, maintain the data centers,
the hardware maintenance itself on the physical machines,
the software support, and even the other data center costs.
So you have data center costs,
the initial investment for the data center itself,
and then you have the ongoing expense for the people

--- Shared Responsibility Model ---

Now, in this chapter, I briefly want to go through the shared responsibility model.
In the last chapter, we talked about the different cloud service models in which we have infrastructure
as a service, we have platform as a service, and we have software as a service.
And in all of those cases I mentioned, you know, what is the responsibility of the cloud provider
and what is our responsibility.
So who maintains what.
So when it comes on to infrastructure as a service, the physical infrastructure is taken care by the
cloud provider.
We are responsible for the virtual machine.
We are responsible for the applications, so on and so forth.
So Microsoft has clearly, you know, kind of told us, okay, what is the shared responsibility model.
But what is Microsoft responsible as the cloud provider and what are we responsible.
What is our responsibilities?
You can go on to this documentation link if you want to learn more about the shared responsibility model.
So again the responsibility depends upon whether we are using the software as a service, whether we're
using platform as a service, whether we're using infrastructure as a service, or whether the company
is hosting everything in their on premises data center.
So as an example, if you look at infrastructure as a service, I mentioned this earlier on.
If you take the case of virtual machines.
So Azure is responsible for maintaining the underlying Azure data center wherein they maintain the servers.
They are responsible for the physical security to the building to the data center itself.
They are responsible for the personnel.
They are responsible for the Ethernet switches, for the power supply, for the cooling, all these
physical layer aspects.
We are responsible for the virtual machine.
So I said we are responsible for what we install on the machine.
We are responsible for maintaining the machine, applying the required patches at the OS level.
We are responsible for the data that we store on the VM itself.
So Microsoft is not responsible for the data that we store on the VM.
We are responsible.
So as a customer, we should be well aware of the clear delineation of responsibilities when it comes
onto Microsoft and when it comes onto ourselves.
You know, when you're looking at these different cloud service models.

--- Azure Traffic Manager ---

Now, in this chapter, I want to have an introduction onto the Azure Traffic Manager service.
So this is a DNS based load balancer.
You have the ability to distribute traffic across public endpoints.
And those endpoints could be spread across different Azure regions.
This is different when it comes onto the Azure Load Balancing service which we had seen earlier on.
So that load balancers traffic at the network level with Azure Traffic Manager you could have public
endpoints.
You could have internet facing endpoints.
This could be within Azure.
You could also have your web applications exposed as endpoints within your on premises data center.
And you can distribute traffic via the use of Azure Traffic Manager.
Now, Azure Traffic Manager directs the client request to the respective endpoints via the use of different
traffic routing methods.
Now there is support for different endpoints.
We have Azure endpoints.
These represent the services that are hosted within Azure.
We have external endpoints.
These represent services that are hosted outside of Azure.
So an example is a public facing web application hosted in your on premises data center.
Then we have nested endpoints.
So these represents a combination of traffic manager profiles.
Then within traffic manager you can configure endpoint monitoring.
Your traffic manager can constantly monitor the endpoint to make sure the endpoint is healthy.
See the traffic manager profile should not route a request onto an unresponsive endpoint.
Now, I said that the Azure Traffic Manager service can actually route traffic based on different routing
methods.
So as an example, there is something known as the priority routing method.
This is what we're going to see when we look at a lab in Azure Traffic Manager.
Let's say that a company has a web application hosted on an Azure Web app.
Let's say that the Azure Web App Service is hosted in the best US location.
Then the company would host the same web application.
The exactly same web application on another Azure Web app.
This time this would be located in the East US location.
When users actually hit the application, the request would go via something known as a Traffic Manager
profile.
I said, here we are going to use the priority routing method.
So when you use this, the Azure Web app that is hosted in the West US location would have a priority
of one.
So when you choose this routing method, the web application running in the west US location would have
a priority of one.
That means all requests from the users would be directed onto this web application.
Now let's consider the extreme condition where in the entire west US location becomes unavailable.
That means the web application is not available.
What the traffic manager profile will do is it will detect that indeed, the web application in West
US is not available based on the priority routing method.
It would now route requests from the users onto the web application running in the east US location.
So this is like having an active passive setup in place for high availability.
Here if one endpoint goes down.
So here our endpoints are the web applications running in their respective regions.
So if one endpoint were to go down traffic manager can actually route requests onto another endpoint.
So this is done in an automated way.
Instead of you trying to monitor whether web applications are going down or not, the traffic manager
profile can automatically detect the health of an endpoint and direct requests accordingly.
And I told you there are many routing methods in place.
This was an example on the priority routing method.
There is another routing method known as the weighted routing method.
Here again you can take the example of two web applications running in respective regions.
Here you can put a weightage for each of the endpoints.
So here for example, if you keep a weight of 50% for both endpoints, then the user traffic would be
equally distributed across the web applications.
This is like having an active active setup in place wherein both endpoints are being used.
So in this chapter, first and foremost I want to give an introduction onto the Traffic Manager service.

--- Azure Front Door ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now in this chapter, I want to give an introduction onto the next service that we're going to be seeing.
And that is the Azure front door service.
There are many kind of traffic distribution services on Azure.
So we have the Azure load balancer.
We have seen the Azure Traffic Manager.
We have Azure front door has another form of delivering web content.
Now primarily Azure Front Door is a content delivery networking service that helps deliver static and
dynamic web content to users across the world in a fast, reliable and secure way.
What does this mean?
Let's say that a company has a web application hosted on an Azure Web app in the West US location.
Now, for users in West US, or those who are close to this region, when they send the request, There
will be, you know, less latency in getting the response back.
See, what we have to understand is, you know, from our laptop, when we send a request on to a resource
on Azure, you have to understand that request is going in the end via our internet services provider.
It's going via the internet.
It's going on to the region, the data center that's hosting the service, the resource that we have.
And then the response comes back.
So there is a distance that our request and response needs to travel.
For users that are closest to West US, they would get the least latency.
That means they would get fast response times.
But for users who are located in places which are far away from west US, Obviously there will be some
latency, right?
They'll there'll be some time before they get the response back.
Normally for web applications you do have some sort of content, you know, delivery mechanism in place
that sits in front of the web application itself.
So with Azure front door, what you can do is you can provision Azure front door in front of the web
application.
But what's the benefit.
And you know because now users instead of directly going to the web application, they would go via
the Azure front door service.
And then Azure front door would route requests onto the web application accordingly.
But what's the benefit of putting something else, something extra in front of our web application?
Azure front door.
Can do is it can make use of these different Point of presence locations that are located by Azure across
the world.
So if we look at the Azure map.
So the Azure map, you know, we do have different data centers, right?
We have different regions.
Right.
And we have different data centers across the world.
And in these data centers Azure hosts their services.
At the same time they have these different network point of presence locations which you can see.
So what happens is if you have a user in a different location and that user sends a request, why Azure
front door or to the web application.
So what Azure front door will do is okay.
It will send the request to the web application.
The web application will send the response back.
Azure front door will send the response Bonds to the user, but at the same time it will cache that
response onto the point of presence location that's closest to the user.
So let's take the example that, you know, you have the web application in West US and you are a user
in UK South.
The user in UK South is making a request to the web application.
So now Azure front door will get the response back from the web application.
It will give it to the user in UK South.
But at the same time Azure front door will take that response.
Give it to the point of presence location in UK South itself.
So there'll be a server.
That response will be cached on that server basically stored on that server.
So now when another user from UK South makes a similar request to get the similar same response back,
instead of the response coming in all the way from Best Us.
The response will be taken from the Point of Presence server in UK South and given to the user.
So that's going to be much more faster.
So this is the primary advantage of using Azure.
Front door has a content delivery networking service wherein you get requests much more faster.
And we can cater to users across the world accessing the web application.
There are other features available with Azure front door.
Firstly, just like Azure Traffic Manager, there are different routing methods in place.
We have that available in Azure front door as well.
Azure front door can route requests based on the performance of endpoints that is also available with
Azure front door, we can couple another service known as the Web Application Firewall.
This helps to protect web applications from known attacks.
Now in the next chapter, I'll just showcase a very simple example on using Azure front door service.

--- Azure DevOps services ---

Welcome back.
Now, in the next set of chapters, I want to showcase some of the features that are available when
it comes onto the Azure DevOps suite of tools.
But before that, for the benefit of students who are not familiar with this entire concept of DevOps
itself, I want to give that brief introduction.
Azure DevOps provides this kind of toolset to manage the various processes.
What's involved in implementing DevOps?
So what is DevOps?
This is a set of practices, principles, tools that are meant to unify the work of software development
and IT operations.
UAV promote collaboration between developers, members of QA and the operations team within.
Automate tasks such as code merging, testing, and deployment.
And then you incorporate regular monitoring of applications and infrastructure.
You know, the entire purpose of DevOps was to have better communication, better collaboration between
development teams, between the testing team and the operations team.
So as an example of this entire flow, just to understand the purpose of DevOps itself, let's go to
the the simple use case scenario.
Let's say a development team is responsible for developing an application.
So they have the first build of the application ready.
They didn't give it on to the testing team.
The testing team performs testing of the entire application.
And now the application is deployed onto a production based environment.
So the development team is happy.
The testing team is happy that they've tested the application, and the operations team is also happy
that the application is in production and working as it should.
So the operations team is now taking over the server operations path, the database operations, managing
the application, etc..
Now let's say that there is a requirement to apply some quick fixes or some quick features on the application.
Normally, when it comes to implementing these new features, these quick fixes, there is a time constraint.
So when building the primary web application and you know the first time when they build the application,
probably the project gets a lot of time, a lot of money, a lot of budget to kind of, you know, have
time and place and money in place for the development team to develop the application for the testing
team to test the application.
But when it comes time to promoting fixes, there's a time constraint.
There is a budget constraint.
Sometimes testing is not conducted in whole for the entire application based on the fixes.
Now, because of this, the operations team can be kind of hesitant to deploy the new changes because
in the end, the operations team are in the line of fire if anything goes wrong.
So if they promote a fix and it causes the application to go down, so the operations team is in the
line of fire.
So there's always this constant problems or tussles between the development team, between the testing
team and the operations team.
The entire purpose of DevOps is to have a smooth flow, smooth collaboration, communication between
your development team, between the operations team, and between the testing team.
Here, when you have changes initially set by customers from the entire requirements, design, development,
that entire cycle, all stakeholders.
So whether it be the dev team, whether it be the testing team, whether it be our users, business
users, whether it be your operations team, all of them are involved.
But apart from this, we you know, there's also a need for automation.
So you want to deliver these changes.
So even though we want collaboration, we also want to deliver these changes in a fast manner.
We want to be able to automate the taking of new updates, the new code updates, build them, test
them automatically, and then promote them on to, let's say, a staging environment and then onto production.
So you have this entire DevOps lifecycle, wherein everything is part of the lifecycle, wherein you
plan for the changes.
You then code the changes accordingly.
You have automated build processes in place.
You have automatic testing in place.
You can then release and deploy those changes, and then you basically monitor those changes in production.
Now as a brief kind of note, we're going to see how we can use the Azure DevOps suite of tools when
it comes on to like planning, when it comes on to building and when it comes on to release.

--- Lab - Azure Key Vault ---

In this chapter, I briefly want to go through the Azure Key Vault service, a very simple yet powerful
service.
The Azure Key Vault is a managed service that can be used for storing secrets, encryption keys, and
certificates.
See for example, let's say you have an application that is connecting onto a database.
Now obviously you need the application to use the database credentials like the username and the password.
You need a safe place to store the user credentials or the database credentials.
What you can do is you can store this as a secret within the Azure Key Vault service.
This is a managed service that can be used to store your secrets.
Your application can then make a secure call on the Azure Key Vault service.
Fetch the value of the password that is the secret, and then use it to connect onto the database.
The keyword service can also be used for storing encryption keys.
There are cases where in applications need to encrypt data.
So in this case they would need to have an encryption key in place.
You can make use of the Azure Key Vault when it comes on to storing and managing the life cycle of the
encryption keys.
Next, when you want to make applications secure through the web protocol, you would want to use certificates
in place.
Now normally if you want to issue certificates you will need to have a certificate manager in place.
Instead of installing and maintaining separate infrastructure for managing the certificates for the
application and for other applications in the company, again, you can use the Azure Key Vault that
can be used for storing and managing certificates.
Now I just want to go through the process of creating an Azure Key Vault.

--- Azure Batch Service ---

Now, in this chapter, I want to give kind of a very brief overview on the Azure Batch Service.
The Azure Batch service is actually used to run large scale, parallel and high performance computing
batch jobs.
Let's say that, you know, you have a requirement wherein, you know, when you take in, let's say
you have a web application that's hosting web content, you know, online videos.
You have users or instructors who are uploading the videos onto the platform.
You want to take in the videos.
You want to process the videos for any sort of content first.
So you want to take the videos in batches.
You want to process the videos you know accordingly.
So what do you normally do is you would spin up, let's say, a set of virtual machines.
You would have your tools, your applications, whatever is required for processing the videos and you
host it on the set of machines.
So in this case, you know, you need the machines.
Your data is basically the videos and you would need to have applications hosted on the machine.
You need, you know, all of this setup in place just for processing the videos itself, for processing
your data.
Now with the Azure Batch service.
This is a managed service.
The Azure Batch service can actually manage these batch processing jobs.
Azure batch can actually manage the pool of virtual machines that you will use for processing your data.
So in our case, I said let's say that you need to process a set of videos.
The batch service can manage the machines that can be used for processing the data.
The batch service can ensure that whatever application you require is installed on the machine, so
it can provision the machines.
Make sure the virtual machines has the applications installed.
You can use Azure Storage accounts when it comes onto the input files.
The storage of the application and the output file.
Azure batch can manage the tasks and the jobs that need to run on the virtual machines itself.
So if you need a manage batch processing service, you can make use of the Azure Batch Service accordingly.

--- What is Microsoft Entra ID ---

And welcome on to the section wherein we are going to look at the security aspect when it comes on to
Azure.
Now, currently when we have been working with Azure Based Services, we have been logging in with our
Azure admin account via the use of Azure Admin account.
We have been deploying resources onto resource groups within our subscription.
But typically in an organization you will have you will not have the admin account that's going ahead
and creating resources within your Azure account.
What you'll normally do is you will want to create users that map onto users within your organization.
So you might have, let's say, a set of users that relate or map onto the IT administrators within
the company, and the IT administrators would use these user credentials to create or work with resources
on the Azure platform as part of the Azure account.
Now, how do you start creating these users?
Well, you can use the identity provider that's located within Azure and that's known as Microsoft Enter.
Microsoft Enter ID is a cloud based identity and access management service.
This identity service can be used not only for Azure.
It can also be used for Microsoft O365 as well, and even other software as a service.
Applications.
So when we log in onto our Azure account using our admin username and password, Microsoft Enter ID
actually verifies our username and password.
So this is the form of authentication.
Now the next step is after we define users in Microsoft enter ID with users again we can give a username
and a password.
The first step is the IT admins can use these user credentials to authenticate themselves onto the Azure
account.
The next step is authorization Are the users given the required permissions to access the resources
within the Azure account you see within a typical organization, you'll be having probably thousands
of resources deployed across various resource groups deployed across various subscriptions within your
Azure account.
Obviously, we need to decide, okay, which users need access to which resources within the Azure account.
So for this we have to give the required permissions.
So the act of seeing that what permissions are given to a particular user, you know the act of authorization.
This also is conducted by Microsoft.
Enter ID.
We can also define identities that map onto applications.
And there's a lot of security features available in Microsoft.
Enter ID like multi-factor authentication auditing and much more.
So in this chapter, first and foremost want to give an introduction onto Microsoft enter ID.

--- Role Based Access Control ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now in the prior chapter, we had seen how we could create a user within Microsoft enter ID, but we
had seen that when we log in onto our Azure account has this new user.
This user has no access to resources within Azure.
Now, by default, when a user is created within Microsoft Azure ID, the user has no permissions whatsoever
to access resources within the Azure account.
In order to give access to users to resources within our Azure account, we need to give them access
via the use of role based access control.
So there's something known as role based access control rules that we can assign to users.
There are various inbuilt roles already available, and using these roles we can assign them onto users.
And once we assign those roles, permissions will be given to the users accordingly.
We also have the ability to create our own custom roles as well.
Now when it comes on to roles so we can assign a role at the individual resource level, we can assign
a role at the resource group level.
Now, if we assign a role at the resource group level, it will apply to all resources within the resource
group, we can assign a role at the subscription level.
If we do this, that role will be applied onto all resources within the subscription.
Now, at a broad level, I'm just showcasing four overarching roles that are available.
But apart from this, there are many other inbuilt roles.
These inbuilt roles actually map onto the various services that are available on the Azure platform.
So these overarching roles are.
So we have the owner role.
If you apply or give the owner role onto a user, you're the user would have complete access to be able
to manage the underlying resources.
The user can also delegate access to other users as well.
If we give the contributor role onto a particular user, you're the user would have complete access
to be able to only manage the resources.
If we give the user admin role here, the user can only delegate access to other users.
And then we have the reader role.
By giving this role, the user would only be able to read the properties of resources.
So in this chapter, first and foremost we want to give an introduction onto role based access control.

And then you can apply role based access control to your security groups.

itself.
It's a very simple process, I said, so I could give, for example, a group name.

A as well.

the mobile device, then it would complete the authentication process.

process is known as multi-factor authentication.
So multi-factor authentication adds that extra layer of security on top of the username and password
to make the authentication process much more secure in nature.
We can enable also multi-factor authentication on a per user basis.
But I said by default it's already enabled for all users in Azure.
We also have the ability to manage the entire process.
While these are something known as conditional access policies.

--- Conditional Access Policies ---

Hi and welcome back.

is Microsoft Enter ID p1 based licenses.

Here.
We can give a policy name.
And then we can define different conditions.
So based on these conditions or based on these signals access can be granted to users accordingly.

access to the Microsoft Admin portal.
Let me click on select.
So we have many applications that we can choose as part of this policy.
We can decide on various conditions based on the user risk level, on the sign in risk level, so on
and so forth.
Since this is a beginner course, we're not going to go through all of those controls.

--- Passwordless authentication ---

In this chapter, I want to give this quick note when it comes on to the passwordless authentication
options that's available for Microsoft enter ID now, when it comes to authenticating onto Microsoft
Enter ID, so normally we use a username and a password for my admin account.
Sometimes when I log in, it sends me a code onto my email.
And we've also looked at the concept of multi-factor authentication.
So when we create a new user in Microsoft Entra.
So by default they would need to set up, let's say the Microsoft Authenticator app has an extra level
of security, that extra level when it comes onto the authentication process.
But there is also that possibility of setting up a passwordless authentication method, wherein you
don't need to enter a username and a password.
Instead, you can use a mechanism that's given on this page where you can directly log into, let's
say, your Azure account using Microsoft Enter ID for example, we can use Windows Hello for business.
So if we do have a windows device that's already registered when it comes onto Windows Hello for business.
So you log into your windows device and then that same login credentials can be used to log into, let's
say your Azure account.

--- Microsoft Entra Domain Services ---

Now, in this chapter, I just want to give kind of a brief overview when it comes on to a service known

for maintaining users.

One of the tools is Microsoft Active Directory.
Now using Microsoft Active Directory, you would install that tool or role or service on a set of machines.
The initial set of machines would be known as domain controllers.
These controllers would have objects that pertain onto the users that are defined within your organization
and other objects such as your machines, your computers, etc..
Now, in case if you can't migrate your users onto Microsoft enter ID, there could be a variety of
reasons, but you want to have an instance of, you know, a domain service on Azure instead of you
installing the required tools on virtual machines hosting domain controllers, you can use a managed
service of Microsoft Entra Domain Services.
This provides many services like domain join group policies.
These features are not available by default in Microsoft Enter ID, so if you want these features,
maybe a company is already using such features in their on premises identity solution.
And if they want to continue using this on Azure and they don't want to maintain the infrastructure,
they can make use of Microsoft Entra Domain Services.

--- Azure DDoS protection ---

Now, in this chapter, I briefly want to go through the Azure DDoS Protection service.
So let's say that a company is hosting a web application.
This could be on a set of Azure VMs.
This could be on an Azure Web app.
Let's consider that this web application is a public facing web application.
So it's exposed onto the internet.
Now obviously if it is exposed on the internet it's also prone to attacks from the internet itself.
So one such attack is the DDoS attack a distributed denial of service attack.
So what happens is you'll have a malicious user that might have bots installed on multiple client devices.
These bots can add a shot, you know, send requests, multiple requests onto our application on our
infrastructure here.
The entire idea is to deny service to other users.
So these bots, I said, will continuously fire requests and try to flood our application with requests
so that the underlying infrastructure is, you know, under so much pressure that it's not able to withstand
the request and hence the application cannot service other users.
So this is an example of a distributed denial of service attack.
For this we can make use of the Azure DDoS protection service.
We have protection available at two levels.
One is at the network level.
So this includes a prediction for a set of public IP resources.
We also have protection for an individual public IP resource as well.
Some of the features available as part of DDoS protection are.
First of all, your application traffic is monitored 24 by seven to check for any sort of attacks.
You get real time metrics when an attack does take place and you get access to a rapid response team.
Briefly, if you go on to the pricing for Azure DDoS protection.
So we have pricing at the network layer.
This is around $3,000 per month.
And there are other charges as well.
And an IP protection level.
The charge is around 1.99 USD per month.
So in this chapter, briefly want to go through the Azure DDoS protection service.

--- Azure Firewall ---

Now, in this chapter, I just want to give kind of a brief overview on another service known as the
Azure Firewall Service.
In an earlier section, we had looked at network security groups that are in place for Azure Virtual
Machines.
The network security groups can filter the incoming and the outgoing traffic at the network layer.
There, you could associate the NSG with either a subnet or a network interface, but normally an organization
would want to have a full fledged firewall appliance in place to protect their resources in networks.
See, what you can do is you can install a firewall appliance from an external vendor onto, you know,
Azure VMs within a virtual network.
These firewalls can be used to look at the incoming traffic, detect any sort of threats or vulnerabilities,
you know, any sort of incoming requests that could pose a threat to the applications running within
a virtual network.
Instead of you managing the infrastructure, managing the appliance.
We can make use of the Azure Firewall Service.
The Azure Firewall Service is a managed service that can be used to look at incoming requests that are
coming in and flowing out of the network.
It can inspect the requests.
It can check if the requests are coming in from bad actors, and if so, it can go ahead and block the
request.
We can deploy this Azure Firewall as a managed service onto our virtual network, so it can be used
to protect our entire virtual network.
The Azure Firewall appliance is actually deployed onto a separate empty subnet within the virtual network,
and it can be used to protect the resources running within the virtual network itself.
So if you want to have a managed service when it comes onto the firewall, we can make use of the Azure
Firewall service accordingly.

--- Concept of Zero Trust ---

In this chapter, I briefly want to go through this concept when it comes to zero trust.
This is basically a security strategy.
It's not a product or a service, but just an approach in designing, you know, kind of security for
your applications or your workloads running on, you know, could be on premise.
It could be on a cloud platform.
Here.
These are based on these three important principles.
First, you have the principle of verify explicitly who always authenticate and authorize the requests
are coming in.
So for example, if you have a, you know, users who have the responsibility of maintaining resources
within your Azure account, make sure that those user accounts are protected.
You can make use of conditional access policies to add that extra layer of security.
You can make sure that you have rules in place via the use of role based access control to give the
required permissions in terms of authorization.
Then use the least privilege access you know.
With role based access control, you can define your own custom roles.
So in those own custom roles, you can only give the permissions that are required to users.
There are other features available in Azure.
For example, there's something known as just in time access, wherein users can ask for permission
to access a virtual machine only when required.
The third principle is to always assume a breach, so try to minimize your blast radius and your segment
access.
For example, if you have a set of Azure Virtual machines, instead of assigning a public IP address
to each and one of the machines which can increase the exposure onto the internet, you might consider
maybe, you know, having a load balancing solution in front of the machines.
If you need connectivity to the machines, you can use other services like Azure Bastion.
So here the concept of zero trust is just make sure that as much as possible, you design security at
the forefront when it comes onto your workloads.
And I said this could be on premise.
This could be on your cloud platform.

--- Azure Service Level Agreements ---

So for example, if I go onto the service of Azure Virtual Machines.
So I'll go on to Virtual Machines Control and click to follow the link.

have the service level agreement.
When it comes on to how do you calculate the uptime for services for virtual machines in an availability
zone?
They first give the definitions of what is the maximum available minutes, what is the downtime and
what is the uptime percentage.
So they guarantee an uptime percentage of generally more than 99%.
When it comes onto their services, in case if they don't meet the service level agreements, then you
get the service credit added onto your account that you can use later on.

--- Notes on managing resource groups ---

In this chapter, I just want to give, you know, some important points when it comes on to resource
groups.
Now, we already know that resource groups are used to logically group your resources.
So when you do deploy a resource on Azure, normally the resource needs to be part of an Azure subscription
and a resource group.
One resource can only be part of one resource group.
There you know we cannot make a resource part of multiple resource groups.
Also when we do create a resource group so you know we can create individual groups itself.
We don't have the ability to nest one resource group within another.
So normally students will ask me these questions as to can we make a resource part of multiple resource
groups.
No you can't.
One resource needs to be part of one resource group.
They also asked me, can we have one resource group that's nested within another?
Again, that is not possible at this point in time.
You can create individual resource groups and then make resources part of the resource groups itself.
Now we also have the ability to move resources within resource groups.
So if I go ahead and create quickly another resource group and let me put this in another location.
So let me try UK South for this particular resource group.
I'll go on to review and create and create the group accordingly.

you know, for a particular reason.

--- Management Groups ---

Now in the chapter, I briefly want to go through management groups.

account.

So here's an example.
Let's say that the company has one subscription for the research department.

let's say, role based access control or Azure policies.
For this we can make use of management groups.
Management groups can be used to manage a set of subscriptions.
It's a pretty simple concept.
By default in your Azure account, you get something known as a root management group.
All of your subscriptions by default come under the root management group.
But what you can do is you can create multiple management groups.
So for example here I could create one termed as business.
And under business I can say that I want my logistics and human resources subscription.
Then I could have another one for it information technology.
And under this I could club my research and my main Azure subscription.
Then once you do this, you can apply both role based access control, which you had seen earlier on,
and even Azure policies at the management group level.
So when you do this, those same roles, those same policies will automatically apply onto subscriptions
under the management group.
So this is ideal when a company manages a lot of subscriptions and they want, let's say these same
roles, these same policies to apply onto these subscriptions.
You can club them under management groups.
Right.
In this chapter briefly want to go through the purpose of using management groups within your Azure

--- Azure resource locks ---

Now, in this chapter, I want to go through a feature known as locking your resources within Azure.
So within Azure you can place a lock on your Azure subscription on your resource group or from resources
to protect them from accidental deletions or modifications.
There are two types of locks that you can create.
One is a cannot delete lock, so your authorized users can read and modify a resource, but they can't
delete the resource.
Another one is a read only lock, so your authorized users can read a resource, but they can't delete
or update it.
So as an example, in Azure, if I go onto all resources, I've deployed a VM as part of a resource
group.
Now if I go on to the VM itself.
So add a resource level.
We have the ability to create locks.
If I go on to settings here We have locks in place.
And I said, I do have the ability to create a lock.
Let me click on add.
So there are two types of locks.
You have a read only and a delete.
Let me create a read only lock.
This is for the virtual machine resource.
Okay.
Now once I've done this, let me go on to the overview.
This particular VM has a particular size standard B-1s.
Let me try to resize the machine.
So I'll go on to availability and scale.
Let me go on to the size and try to resize the machine.
I'll choose another size.
And let's click on the resize operation.
Let's see what happens.
So we're getting an error.
We're getting an error saying that we cannot perform the write operation.
We cannot change the status or the size of the VM because there is a lock.
There is a read only lock.
That means no changes.
No modifications are allowed onto the resource itself.
So if you want to protect your resources from accidental modification or deletion, we can go ahead
and make use of resource locks in order to make sure that you can perform operations on resources again
on this particular resource.
So you can go back onto the locks and we can delete the lock.
So note that or remember that once you have a lock in place, if you want to release the lock code and
delete the lock for a resource, you can have multiple locks.
So you can have one read only lock and a delete lock.
That's possible.
We can also create locks at not only the resource level, but at the resource group level and at the
subscription level as well.
So if I go on to the resource group and under the resource group.
If I go on to settings, if I go on to locks here we have the ability to create a lock at the resource
group level.
So if I go ahead and just create a read only lock and click on okay.
Now this has been done at the resource group level.
If I go on to my VM.
If I go on to locks you will see the lock in place.
It has been inherited from the resource group.
So if you go on to all resources right.
If you go on to the public IP address here again we have under settings.
If you go on to locks you will see a lock in place.
And again this lock has been inherited from the resource group.
If we need to release the lock we'll have to go on to the resource group again and go on to the locks
and delete the lock accordingly.
Right.
So in this chapter I want to go through the process of resource locks.

--- Lab - ARM templates ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now in this chapter I want to go through the usage of arm based templates.
So ARM stands for Azure Resource Manager.
Now when working with Azure Based Services when we deploy resources.
So let's say we are deploying a storage account.
We are deploying an Azure VM.
What we do is we go onto the Azure portal.
We then kind of create a resource based on the service.
We are presented with a wizard.
In the wizard we go ahead and configure the different aspects and then we deploy our resources accordingly.
Now let's say that there is a requirement to kind of build a test environment that consists of a set
of machines, that consists of an Azure SQL database that consists of storage accounts.
You need to provision this test environment on a regular basis.
So let's say you have an application that requires these resources.
You want to test the newer version of the application and hence you need to provision a test environment
on a temporary basis.
Deploy the application.
Test the application.
Once it is done, go ahead and delete the infrastructure.
Write for that environment, for the test environment and then the next time around when you need to
again test a newer version of the application itself, you have to provision the test environment again.
Again you have to provision the VMs.
You have to provision the storage account, provision the Azure SQL database.
Instead of going to the Azure portal over and over again, going through the wizards of deploying the
resources, what you can do is you can make use of ARM based templates.
ARM is infrastructure as code.
You if you have the requirement of repeatedly deploying resources, deploying infrastructure, you can
define your infrastructure as code and then submit that code onto Azure and Azure can provision that
infrastructure for you.
The other advantage is when you define your infrastructure as code, you can store that code in a version
control tool to maintain different versions of the ARM template.
You define the ARM template in JSON, that is JavaScript object notation.

documentation has a lot of examples.

Again this is a domain specific language that can be used to again deploy Azure resources.

--- Revision ---

Now in this chapter, let's have again a revision based on some of the concepts that we have learned
in this particular section.
So first is the ability of a company to create multiple subscriptions.
Now for us we've only been working with one subscription but that's fine.
But a company does have the ability to create multiple subscriptions as part of their Azure account.
So they could create individual subscriptions for each department within their organization so they
could track costs accordingly.
You can also make use of something known as resource tags to add metadata or additional key value pairs
to your resources.
You can also do it for your resource groups.
We can use management groups if you want to manage multiple subscriptions.
Now we can build and manage Azure resources via the use of the Azure Portal.
During this entire course, we've been actually creating and working with Azure Resources via the portal
itself.
But we can also build and manage resources via other tools.
So we can use the Azure command line interface.
We can make use of Azure PowerShell modules.
We can also deploy resources via the use of Azure Resource Manager templates and bicep based templates.
We can manage Azure based services from any client based machine.
So whether it be Linux, whether it be windows, whether you have an Android device, whether you have
Mac OS, in the end, you just need a browser within the browser.
You can use the Azure portal.
When it comes to Azure CLI and PowerShell, there is support for Linux.
That is support for windows.
There's support for Mac OS.
With Azure Resource Manager templates, you can define your infrastructure as code.
Here the template is in JSON format.
You can define the deployment of multiple resources within a template.
And you can maintain also different versions of your template using let's say a version control tool.
You can deploy the template either using the Azure portal, or you could even use tools such as Azure
PowerShell to deploy your templates.
Bicep templates can be used to deploy resources by using a domain specific language.
We looked at the Azure Advisor tool.
This is a cloud assistant that helps you to follow the best practices when it comes onto deployment
of Azure Resources.
Here you can get recommendations that are divided into different categories.
So we have categories in terms of reliability, security, performance, cost and operational excellence.
We can also generate alerts using Azure Advisor tools.
So whenever there are new recommendations via this tool you can get alerts accordingly.
And Azure Advisor has support for a variety of Azure based services so it can monitor those Azure based
services and give you recommendations accordingly.

--- Revision - General Concepts ---

Instructor: Now, in this chapter,
there's some revision on some general concepts.
We first have Azure regions.
These are comprised of data centers
that are deployed with latency-defined perimeter.
We have Azure availability zones.
These are physically separate locations
within an Azure region that are tolerant to local failures.
If you need to protect resources
against data center level failures,
then you can deploy your resources
across availability zones.
Now, please note that Azure availability zones
will not replicate your data automatically,
this you need to do on your own.
You can deploy machines based on both Windows or Linux
onto availability zones.
We have Azure resource groups.
This can be used to logically group your resources together.
A resource can be part of only one resource group.
You can't nest resource groups together.
The resource and the resource group
can be part of different regions.
When you assign tags onto a resource group,
they don't apply to resources in the resource group itself.
We have Azure subscriptions.
This is used from a billing perspective.
You can create multiple subscriptions.
A resource can only belong to one subscription.
If you have multiple subscriptions in place,
you cannot merge them together.
And you can also use one Microsoft account
to manage multiple Azure subscriptions.
We have Azure Resource Manager.
This is the main part of the Azure ecosystem.
This helps to deploy resources to Azure
in a consistent manner.
We have Resource Manager templates.
This can be used to automate the deployment of resources.
We have management groups.
This can be used to manage multiple subscriptions.
And we have resource tags.
This is used to add metadata onto your resources.
You can filter resources and costs based on resource groups.
So in this chapter, I want to have a quick revision
on the general concepts.
